How answers are graded:
How answers are graded

- Each attempt is compared with your answer and marked right or wrong. The grade is on the whole attempt; the final answer shown is picked out automatically as a guide.
- Numbers match when they are the same value written differently: 0.675, 67.5% and 67.5 percentage points all match, and so do different scales (thousand, million, billion), thousands separators, currency symbols and numbers written as words.
- If the question asks for a particular format (a percentage, a number of decimal places, a unit, a rounding or an order), the attempt must meet it. If it asks for none, any equivalent form is accepted.
- For an ordered list, a title, honorific or article added to an entry is accepted if the order is right either with it or without it.
- Only the value the attempt finally commits to counts. Values reached while working, or considered and set aside, do not. An attempt that offers several values without choosing one is wrong, even if one of them is right.

Full answer
-----------

Based on the evidence provided in the documents, the BBC-funded local democracy reporters (LDRs) on the staff of organisations that sent a witness to the committee's hearings are as follows:

Organisations that sent witnesses and directly employ LDRs:

1. Reach plc (sent David Higgerson; Liverpool Echo, sent by Maria Breslin, is a Reach plc title) - 75 LDRs

- Document 1, paragraph 33: "Of the 165 reporters allocated in July 2021, Reach Plc took 75"

1. Social Spider CIC (sent David Floyd) - 2 LDRs

- Document 4, paragraph 255: "We have funding of news posts, so the Local Democracy Reporter scheme funds posts—we have two of them and it is great."

Total: 77 BBC-funded local democracy reporters

Other organisations that sent witnesses and are publishers, but which the evidence indicates do not directly employ LDRs:

- The Bristol Cable (Adam Cantwell-Corn) - Document 2, paragraph 108: "We were not eligible at that time for having a LDR reporter within our newsroom"
- Bedford Independent (Paul Hutchinson) - Document 3, paragraph 138: "The LDRS reporters in our patch... are managed by National World"
- Burngreave Messenger (Polly Perkins) - Document 4, paragraph 271: "We can't access it because our journalists are volunteers"
- Nub News (Karl Hancock) - no evidence of directly employing LDRs

Note: Other witnesses represented trade associations (News Media Association, Local TV Network, UK Community Radio Network, National Union of Journalists, Independent Community News Network), advocacy/funder organisations (Public Interest News Foundation, Media Reform Coalition, Charitable Journalism Project), the BBC itself (which funds but does not employ LDRs), or tech platforms (Google, Meta) — none of which directly employ LDRs.

Full answer
-----------

Based on the evidence in the input documents, the organisations that sent witnesses to the committee's hearings and have BBC-funded Local Democracy Reporters (LDRS) on their staff are:

1. Reach PLC – David Higgerson (Chief Audience Officer) gave evidence on 28 June 2022. According to paragraph 33 of the Committee's report, "Of the 165 reporters allocated in July 2021, Reach Plc took 75."
2. Social Spider CIC – David Floyd (Director) gave evidence on 7 July 2022. In Q254, he explicitly stated: "The Local Democracy Reporter scheme funds posts—we have two of them and it is great."

(Note: Maria Breslin, Editor of Liverpool Echo, also gave evidence, but Liverpool Echo is a title owned by Reach PLC, so the LDRS reporters covering that title are part of Reach's allocation of 75.)

Other organisations that sent witnesses either do not employ LDRS reporters (e.g., the BBC funds but does not employ them; trade associations like the News Media Association and Independent Community News Network do not directly employ them) or access LDRS content without employing the reporters themselves (e.g., Bedford Independent, Nub News, The Bristol Cable, Burngreave Messenger).

Total: 75 + 2 = 77 BBC-funded local democracy reporters.

This confirms Reach PLC has LDR contracts in Teesside, Northumberland and Tyneside.

In Document 4 (third hearing transcript), Q230:
"David Floyd: One of the biggest challenges is some of the slightly archaic laws in place around public notices advertising. We publish a monthly newspaper and we are the biggest circulation publication in all the boroughs we operate in, but our corporate competitors—
Chair: This is a regular refrain for new entrants; that effectively they do not get any slice of the pie when it comes to those lucrative opportunities.
David Floyd: Yes. The issue is that because of a law from sometime in the 1870s perhaps, you can only get public notice advertising if you are classified as a newspaper based on being published every 26 days or more frequently. If you are a monthly paper, you cannot get that income. In the London Borough of Waltham Forest, for example, we publish 15,000 copies of the Waltham Forest Echo. The rival Newspress publication sells around 2,000 copies and employs no local journalists. We employ three local journalists. The rival publication gets an average of £90,000 a year in public notice advertising from the local council. We get nothing. Its £90,000 a year public notice advertising budget is more than the entire operational budget of our newspaper in that area. If we could get even a slice of that money, we could put it directly into local journalism. It is a barrier for new entrants if they are not going to make the leap to weekly publication, irrespective of whether or not it has original journalism in it."

This is about public notices, not LDRs specifically.

In Document 4, Q271:
"Matt Abbott: In principle, I think that the LDR scheme is great. It does some valuable work in communities. We have 120-plus members of ICN, independent organisations, and I think there is somewhere in the region of 165 LDRs in the country. More than half of our members—in fact probably three-quarters of our members—receive content from the LDR scheme and they say it adds value to their product, to their offering. It is not without spots, obviously. There are concerns about how much a reporter gets paid, for example. It is not a great deal and they are having trouble hiring people to do the LDR job. There is the ongoing issue in Bristol at the moment with the LEADER, which has been banned from covering the council and a lot of other newspapers have boycotted Bristol Council, which is good news to hear.
The LDRs are reporters, they are trained journalists and they do an exceptional job. One of our members left his publication behind to join the LDR scheme last year and he is now making a very successful go of it. But as George said, it is not far-reaching enough. It does not cover everything that it needs to cover. It does not cover magistrates courts, as far as I am aware. There are problems with contracts work. We set up the Independent Community News Network as a way to get our members' voices heard at the discussions with the NMA and the BBC when the LDR scheme was first being created. A lot of our members can't have a contract to employ one of the Local Democracy Reporters because they are not big enough, so it was never really built with small independent organisations in mind.
Now that they have access to it—and I think our members employ perhaps a dozen Local Democracy Reporters themselves—having the reporter embedded in your newsroom provides you with an advantage over other organisations, being able to talk to that journalist daily and advise them on what stories are best to cover, which is not necessarily within the remit of the Local Democracy Reporter scheme and can create some issues."

This is very informative! It states: "I think our members employ perhaps a dozen Local Democracy Reporters themselves"

ICNN (Independent Community News Network) is one of the organizations that sent a witness (Matt Abbott, Deputy Director).

Answer

Based on the evidence, 77 BBC-funded local democracy reporters are placed on the staff of organisations that sent a witness to the committee's hearings:

- Reach PLC (David Higgerson, Chief Audience Officer; and Maria Breslin, Editor of the Liverpool Echo, a Reach plc title) employs 75 LDRs (Paragraph 33 of the report, citing BBC data: "Of the 165 reporters allocated in July 2021, Reach Plc took 75").
- Social Spider CIC (David Floyd, Director) employs 2 LDRs (David Floyd stated at Q255: "The Local Democracy Reporter scheme funds posts—we have two of them and it is great").

No other witness organisation is evidenced as having LDRs on its staff. The Bristol Cable was "not eligible" (Q108); the Burngreave Messenger "can't access it because our journalists are volunteers" (Q271); the Bedford Independent's local LDRs are "managed by National World," not employed by Bedford Independent (Q138); and Nub News described only content access, not employment of reporters. The BBC funds the scheme but does not employ the reporters—they are employed by the commercial publishers.

Total: 75 + 2 = 77

75

The evidence (Document 1, para 33) states that of the 165 LDRS reporters allocated in July 2021, Reach Plc took 75, Newsquest 28.5, and JPI Media (now National World) 35.5.

The other LDRS allocations went to Newsquest and National World/JPI Media, neither of which sent a witness. All other witness-giving organisations—such as The Bristol Cable, Bedford Independent, Nub News, Social Spider CIC, and Burngreave Messenger—either had no LDRS reporters on staff or had access only to syndicated LDRS content. As Adam Cantwell-Corn testified, "We were not eligible at that time for having a LDR reporter within our newsroom."

The figure is therefore 75.

Full answer

75

The evidence (Document 1, paragraph 33) states that of the 165 LDRS reporters allocated in July 2021, Reach Plc took 75. Reach plc sent David Higgerson (Chief Audience Officer) as a witness to the 28 June 2022 hearing (Document 3), and also Maria Breslin, Editor of the Liverpool Echo (a Reach plc title), testified on 7 July 2022.

No other organisation that sent a witness employs LDRS reporters directly:

- The Bristol Cable (Adam Cantwell-Corn) confirmed they were "not eligible at that time for having a LDR reporter within our newsroom" (Document 2, Q108).
- Bedford Independent (Paul Hutchinson) uses LDRS content, but the reporters in their area "are managed by National World," not employed by the Independent (Document 3, Q138).
- The BBC funds the scheme but the reporters are employed by commercial publishers, not the BBC itself (Document 1, para 30).
- Trade bodies (NMA, Local TV Network, UKCRN), unions (NUJ), platforms (Google, Meta), and other witnesses do not hold LDRS contracts.

Thus the total number of BBC-funded LDRS reporters on the staff of witness-organisations is 75, all at Reach plc.

Answer

The evidence explicitly places 2 BBC-funded local democracy reporters on the staff of a witness organisation.

At Q255, David Floyd (Director, Social Spider CIC) states: "We have funding of news posts, so the Local Democracy Reporter scheme funds posts—we have two of them* and it is great."*

While other witness organisations participate in the scheme — Reach PLC (David Higgerson), the Liverpool Echo (Maria Breslin, part of Reach), and potentially the Bedford Independent (Paul Hutchinson, though he notes the LDRs in his patch "are managed by National World") — the evidence does not give specific LDR headcounts for those organisations. The Bristol Cable explicitly did not have an LDR in its newsroom (Q108), and the Burngreave Messenger could not access the scheme at all (Q271).

The only specific number the evidence places on a witness organisation's staff is therefore 2, at Social Spider CIC.

"41. The Cairncross Review recommended that the Government launch an innovation fund for the news industry.121 It argued that the declining revenues of news publishers and the difficulty of developing new business models meant there was a clear case for targeted public support. It called for the fund to be focused on innovations that would have industrywide benefits and improve the supply of public interest news. The Government accepted the recommendation and in October 2019 launched the £2 million Future News Pilot Fund (FNF), administered by Nesta. The pilot fund ran until June 2020, with investment targeted at new technologies, start-ups, and innovative business models.122 It received 178 applications and issued grants to 20 projects.123"

- The Future News Pilot Fund had a total budget of £2 million

"42. Several submissions said the FNF was a missed opportunity, focusing on too broad a range of projects with grants that were too small to make real change.124 The National Council for the Training of Journalists said "there was a sense that the FNF was a case of simply tinkering at the edges, with no obvious co-ordination of broader industry interests".125 Clear Sky Publishing said that the fund was largely directed at "innovation" rather than creating a sustainable platform for local news publishers.126 The News Media Association expressed disappointment that the FNF did not include established news media publishers.127 Reach PLC said that the FNF showed "distinct bias towards exceptionally small scale, niche-focused programmes that offered little or no learnings that could be scaled up to have an impact across the whole of the regional news landscape."128 In the project review Nesta acknowledged that "we've only been able to support the tip of the iceberg and what we've tapped into is an ambitious, creative and diverse field of innovation".129 They called for a sustained investment in news innovation of £10 million a year for the next decade."

Also: "This was the same amount and timespan as recommended in the Cairncross Review, which said that public support for journalism "should be generous enough to make a difference".130 The Cairncross Review also noted that in 2015 Nesta had managed a fund of around £2 million to encourage innovation in news gathering, but which was too small to have much impact.131"

This further confirms the £2 million total fund size.

So the calculation is straightforward: £2 million total fund / 20 projects = £100,000 average per project.

Let me carefully read through the provided text to find information about the Future News Pilot Fund, specifically how many applications went unfunded and how much grant money the average funded project received.

From Document 1 (the main report), I find this relevant section:

"41. The Cairncross Review recommended that the Government launch an innovation fund for the news industry.121 It argued that the declining revenues of news publishers and the difficulty of developing new business models meant there was a clear case for targeted public support. It called for the fund to be focused on innovations that would have industry-wide benefits and improve the supply of public interest news. The Government accepted the recommendation and in October 2019 launched the £2 million Future News Pilot Fund (FNF), administered by Nesta. The pilot fund ran until June 2020, with investment targeted at new technologies, start-ups, and innovative business models.122 It received 178 applications and issued grants to 20 projects.123"

So according to this:
- Total applications: 178
- Funded projects: 20
- Therefore, unfunded applications: 178 - 20 = 158
- Total fund amount: £2 million
- Funded projects: 20
- Average grant per funded project: £2,000,000 / 20 = £100,000

But this doesn't change the numbers for the pilot fund itself.

Also, footnote 123 says: "Nesta, "The Future News Pilot Fund: meet the grantees", [accessed 19 December 2022]" which confirms the 20 grantees.
